Let me think about the problem structure more carefully.

C₅ = max_k ∫ h(x)(1-h(x+k)) dx

For k=0: ∫ h(1-h) dx. To minimize this, we want h to be as close to 0 or 1 as possible (binary), because h(1-h) is maximized at h=0.5 and minimized at h∈{0,1}.

But for other k, we want h(x) and h(x+k) to be 1 simultaneously (so 1-h(x+k) is 0 where h(x) is 1). This means pairs at distance k should both be 1.

Wait, we minimize the max. So for each k, we want ∫ h(x)(1-h(x+k)) dx to be small. For a given k, h(x)(1-h(x+k)) is nonzero when h(x)=1 and h(x+k)=0. To make this small for all k, we want: whenever h(x)=1, then h(x+k)=1 for "many" k... but that's impossible for all k unless h is constant 1, which violates the integral constraint.

Actually wait. Let me reconsider. We want MIN over h of MAX over k of the integral. The max picks the worst k. So we need all the integrals (over k) to be small.

Hmm, for k=0, ∫ h(1-h) is small when h is near-binary. But if h is binary, then... let me think about what a binary h gives.

f_0 = ∫ h(1-h) = 0 for binary (since h is 0 or 1).

Better approach for local search: maintain the correlation array incrementally. When we change h[i] and h[j] by +d and -d (keeping sum constant), we can update the correlate array in O(n) instead of O(n²).

Let me think. The correlate c[m] = ∑_x h[x] * (1-h)[x + (m - (n-1))] roughly. Actually np.correlate(a, v, mode='full')[k] = ∑_n a[n+k] * v[n] (with appropriate indexing). Let me define it clearly.

np.correlate(a, v, 'full')[k] = ∑_n a[k + n] v[n] for the valid range, padded at edges.

Let me define c_full with length 2n-1. c_full[k] = ∑_n a[n] * v[n + k - (n-1)] ... this indexing is confusing. Let me just think of it as cross-correlation.

The key: c depends linearly on the products h[a]·(1-h[b]). If I change h[i] by δi and h[j] by δj, each c[k] changes by terms involving h[i], h[j]. Specifically:

c[k] = ∑_{p} h[p] (1 - h[p + k]) (for appropriate p range).

∂c[k]/∂h[i] = (1 - h[i+k Contribution...]) ... Let me just say: c[k] is a bilinear form. Changing h[i] affects every term in c where i appears as p or as p+k.

Incremental update: Let's define c[k] = ∑_p h[p]·q[p+k] where q = 1-h. Then q[p] = 1 - h[p], so ∂q[p]/∂h[i] = -δ_{p,i}.

c[k] = ∑_p h[p] q[p+k].
∂c[k]/∂h[i] = ∂/∂h[i] ∑_p h[p] q[p+k] = (∂h[i]/∂h[i]) q[i+k] + ∑_p h[p] ∂q[p+k]/∂h[i]
= q[i+k] + ∑_p h[p] (-δ_{p+k, i}) = q[i+k] - h[i-k] (if i-k in range, 0 else).

So ∂c[k]/∂h[i] = (1 - h[i+k]) - h[i-k].

Thus if h[i] → h[i] + δ, then c[k] → c[k] + δ·(1-h[i+k]) - δ·h[i-k] (for each k), plus we must update h[i] in the q terms... wait, but this is the derivative at the current point; a finite change δ gives a change δ·∂c/∂h[i] plus higher order? No—c is bilinear in h, so ∂c/∂h[i] is linear in h (it's 1 - h[i+k] - h[i-k], which is linear in h). So the change for a finite δ is:

Δc[k] from changing h[i]→h[i]+δ:
= (h[i]+δ - h[i]) · [terms] ... let me just recompute.

c[k] uses h[i] in the term p=i: h[i]·q[i+k], and uses h[i] in q when p+k = i, i.e., p = i-k: term h[i-k]·q[i], where q[i] = 1 - h[i]. So:

c[k] = ... + h[i]·q[i+k] + h[i-k]·q[i] + ...

Change h[i]→h[i]+δ: q[i] → q[i]-δ (since q[i]=1-h[i]).
- term h[i]·q[i+k] → (h[i]+δ)·q[i+k]: change +δ·q[i+k].
- term h[i-k]·q[i] → h[i-k]·(q[i]-δ): change -δ·h[i-k].

So Δc[k] = δ·(q[i+k] - h[i-k]) = δ·((1-h[i+k]) - h[i-k]). ✓ matches derivative (no higher-order terms because bilinear and we only change one variable, the cross term... wait actually both terms are linear in h[i], so the finite change equals the derivative times δ exactly). Good.

So incremental update is O(n) per h[i] change. 

For a pair move (i: +d, j: -d), Δc = d·G_i - d·G_j where G_i[k] = (1-h[i+k])-h[i-k], G_j[k] = (1-h[j+k])-h[j-k]. So O(n) to update all c. Then compute max(c)*dx. O(n). So each pair evaluation is O(n) = 200, vs O(n²) = 40000 before. 200× speedup. 

Alternatively, I can use FFT-based correlate. np.correlate is direct. Using FFT, correlate = ifft(fft(a)·conj(fft(v))) or something. For n=200, n² = 40000 direct is fine. For larger n, FFT helps. But incremental update is O(n) per move which is best for local search.

Actually, the smoothness: The objective is max over k of f_k, where each f_k is bilinear in h. The max of bilinear is convex in h? No. max of linear functions is convex, but bilinear is not convex. So it's a nonconvex minimax. However, fixing everything except one variable, it's still nonconvex in general. Hmm. Actually max_k f_k where f_k is bilinear—considering h as the variable, f_k = ∫ h(x)(1-h(x+k)) dx. For fixed other parts, as a function of a single h[i], it's linear (since only linear terms in h[i]): f_k = ... + h[i]·(1-h[i+k]) + h[i-k]·(1-h[i]) = ... + h[i] - h[i]h[i+k] + h[i-k] - h[i]h[i-k]. This is linear in h[i]: coefficient of h[i] is (1 - h[i+k] - h[i-k]) + ... wait: h[i] - h[i]h[i+k] - h[i]h[i-k] = h[i](1 - h[i+k] - h[i-k]). So yes linear in h[i]. So max_k of linear functions in h[i] is convex in h[i]! So coordinate minimization is well-defined (convex in each coordinate). 

So we can do coordinate descent: for each i, given other h fixed, minimize max_k f_k over h[i]∈[0,1] while adjusting to keep sum constraint. This is a 1D convex minimization problem! Actually, we need to keep the sum constraint, so when we change h[i], we must change another h[j]. That breaks the coordinate-wise convexity.

Hmm. Alternatively, we can use a Lagrange multiplier for the sum constraint and do unpenalized coordinate updates then project. Or do pair updates (i,j) which keeps 2D but that's not convex in 2D either.

Alternative: Use the fact that with the sum constraint, we can formulate as: minimize max_k f_k subject to ∑h = n/2, 0≤h≤1. This is a min-max linear programming-ish problem but bilinear makes it nonconvex.

Let me just use np.correlate to GET c and then figure out the incremental delta by matching. Alternatively—I can derive the delta purely in terms of "which products change" and be agnostic to the index convention:

c is a sum over products h[p]·q[r] for some pairing (p,r) that depends on the lag. When I change h[i] by δ (and q[i] = 1-h[i] by -δ), the products that involve h[i] (as the h) or q[i] (as the q) change. Specifically, for each lag, the product pairs (p, r) with r = p + lag or p = r + lag. The term involving h[i]: it appears as h[p=i] paired with q[r=i+lag] for each lag (i.e., p=i). And it appears as q[r=i] paired with h[p=i-lag] for each lag (r=i). 

So Δc[for the Lag L term] from h[i]→h[i]+δ:
- term where h is at index i: h[i]q[i+L] → (h[i]+δ)q[i+L]: change +δ q[i+L].
- term where q is at index i: h[i-L]q[i] → h[i-L](q[i]-δ): change -δ h[i-L], but careful: the "h[i-L]" is the pair's h-factor, which is h[i-L] (unchanged), and q[i] changes. But wait, is there double counting? The product h[i]q[i] (L=0, p=i=r=i) appears only once. When we change h[i] and q[i], the product h[i]q[i] → (h[i]+δ)(q[i]-δ) = h[i]q[i] + δ q[i] - δ h[i] - δ². So change = δ q[i] - δ h[i] - δ². Our formula: +δ q[i+0] - δ h[i-0] = δ q[i] - δ h[i]. Missing the -δ². 

Ah, the bilinear has a cross term when it's the SAME index (L=0). Because h[i] and q[i] are not independent (q[i]=1-h[i], correlated). So the finite delta for the L=0, p=r=i term is (h[i]+δ)(1-h[i]-δ) - h[i](1-h[i]) = [h[i] - δh[i] + δ - δ²] - [h[i] - h[i]²]... let me just compute: h[i](1-h[i]) = h - h². (h+δ)(1-h-δ) = (h+δ)(1-h) - (h+δ)δ = h(1-h) + δ(1-h) - δh - δ² = h - h² + δ - δh - δh - δ² = h-h² + δ(1-2h) - δ². So change = δ(1-2h[i]) - δ² = δ(1-h[i] - h[i] - ... ) wait 1-2h = (1-h) - h. So = δ((1-h[i]) - h[i]) - δ² = δ q[i] - δ h[i] - δ².

For L≠0 (p≠r), the two indices are distinct, so changing h[i] only affects the term where one factor is at index i, the other at a different index (unchanged). So change is exactly δ q[i+L] (for p=i) and -δ h[i-L] (for r=i), no δ².

So the incremental update:
Δc[L] = δ q[i+L] - δ h[i-L] - (δ² if L=0 else 0).

Fast local search with incremental c:

def make_corr(h): 
    q = 1.0 - h
    c = np.correlate(h, q, 'full')
    return c

Note np.correlate returns float array length 2n-1. c[b]*dx.

But I'll maintain c and h together. For a candidate move changing h[i] (delta_di), h[j] (delta_dj), compute new_max = max(c + dC) where dC combines contributions. Let me compute dC as a full-length vector:

dC[i-change] = for each k: contribution. Let me write a function that, given h, c, and (i, di), returns the dC array for changing h[i] by di (without -δ² unless self). Then for a pair, dC_total = dC_i + dC_j. Then new_c = c + dC_total; new_max = new_c.max()*dx.

But building dC arrays is O(n) each, so O(n) total per candidate, fine.

Actually, I realize I can be even smarter. The max of new_c = max over k of [c[k] + di*A_i[k] + dj*A_j[k]] where A_i[k] is the per-unit delta (G matrix column). This is a 1-parameter max problem if I'm just scaling di. For a fixed pair (i,j) and direction, moving along the line h_i → h_i + t, h_j → h_j - t (t real, keeping sum), new_c[k] = c[k] + t·G_i[k] - t·G_j[k] = c[k] + t·(G_i[k]-G_j[k]). So new_max(t) = max_k (c[k] + t D[k]) where D = G_i - G_j. This is a convex piecewise-linear function of t (max of linear). We want the smallest t in [tmin, tmax] (feasibility: h_i+t∈[0,1], h_j-t∈[0,1]) that minimizes new_max(t). This is a 1D convex minimization! 

So for each pair (i,j), the best t (step) minimizing max overlap is found by a convex 1D problem. Even better, and we don't need fixed deltas—we find the optimal step automatically.

G_i[k] = (1-h[i+dt]) valid ? 1-h[i+dt] : 0 minus (h[i-dt] valid ? h[i-dt]:0), where dt = k-(n-1). Wait, from our formula: dC for changing h[i] by 1 unit (di=1) is: for each k, G_i[k] = q[i+dt] (if i+dt valid) - h[i-dt] (if i-dt valid), and if dt=0, additionally -1 (the -δ² becomes -1·di², but for the linear-in-t we drop the constant/δ² term... wait for a pair along line h_i+t, h_j-t, the δ for i is +t, for j is -t. The -t² cross term applies only at dt=0 for each, so contributes -t² (for i) - (-t)² (for j)... wait both would contribute -t² each at dt=0? The cross -δ² happens at the self-term (dt=0). For i, at dt=0 (k=n-1): dC includes -di² = -t². For j, at dt=0: dC includes -dj² = -t². So total quadratic term -2t² added to new_c[k=n-1] only. So new_c[k] = c[k] + t D[k] for k≠n-1, and new_c[n-1] = c[n-1] + t D[n-1] - 2t². So it's not exactly linear but convex still. Good, convex in t. Local search details:
- State: h (n), c (2n-1). Feasibility: 0≤h≤1, ∑h=n/2.
- c5 = max(c)*dx.
- For each pass:
   - For each pair (i,j) (i<j), find the best t (step in [-? , +?]) where we do h[i]+=t, h[j]-=t (and also the reverse, covered by t sign), subject to 0≤h[i]+t, h[j]-t≤1, that minimizes max(c + t·(G_i - G_j) with the -2t² cross at k=n-1). If improvement > tol, apply the best t.
   - Repeat passes until no improvement.

To be fast, for each pair I'll evaluate a small set of t: the full allowed range endpoints and the "kink" points. Actually, since new_max(t) is convex piecewise-linear (plus tiny quadratic), the minimizer is either at a kink or where a slope crosses zero, or boundary. Doing full golden section per pair is expensive. Instead, I'll:
   - Compute D = G_i - G_j (length 2n-1), and also handle the cross term.
   - The current c5 entries: c. The new_max(t) = max_k (c[k] + t D[k]) for k≠n-1, and c[n-1]+t D[n-1]-2t² for k=n-1.
   - A quick way: the improvement is limited; let me just do a cheap ternary search over t in the feasible interval with ~20 evals. Each eval is a max of an array (compute c + t*D vectorized → O(n) but vectorized, ~fast). 20 evals × O(n) vectorized = 20n ≈ 20·200=4000 ops per pair, ×n²=20000 pairs = 8e7 per pass, but vectorized per pair... each pair does 20 vector ops of length 2n-1. 20000 pairs × 20 = 400000 vector ops of length 400. In C that's 400000×400 = 1.6e8 element-ops ≈ 0.16s if memory-bandwidth bound ~1GB/s... actually more like a few hundred MHz... let me estimate ~0.2-0.5s per pass. Times ~50 passes = 10-25s per delta cycle. Hmm. And we might do a few cycles. Might be too slow for the whole budget across multiple n.

Let me reduce: Do NOT full ternary per pair. Instead, use an adaptive fixed-step with incremental updates: pick a small step d, for pair (i,j) try h[i]+=d,h[j]-=d → new_max via c+Δ (single vector op), and reverse. If improves, apply (update c incrementally, O(n)). This is the classic operator sequence. With incremental O(n) update and O(n) max (vectorized), per candidate = 2 vector ops (one for + direction compute new_c, max) ~ O(n). Per pair 2 directions → ~4 vector ops length 2n. n²=20000 pairs → 80000 vector ops/pass ×400 length... same ballpark. Hmm, 80000 vector length-400 ops = 3.2e7 element ops per pass ~0.03s (C). × (passes). If 20 passes per step-size and 5 step sizes = 100 passes = ~3s per resolution. That's very doable across multiple resolutions and restarts!

Wait, 80000 vector ops per pass? Per pair we do, for direction +d: new_c_plus = c + dC_plus (one vector add+scale, length 2n-1=399); m = new_c_plus.max(). That's ~2 vector ops (scale+add, and max) length 399. For direction -d (i.e., h[i]-=d, h[j]+=d): another 2 ops. So 4 vector ops (length ~399) per pair. n²/2 = 20000 pairs → 80000 vector ops per pass, each 399 elements → ~3.2e7 element operations in C per pass. At ~1e9 element-ops/s (vectorized C, memory bound maybe lower), ~0.03s per pass. For 100 passes, ~3s. 

But if a move improves, we APPLY it: update h and c incrementally (O(n) vector op) — cheap. And we set improved=true, continue. Since we apply mid-pass, the c changes, subsequent pairs use updated c. Fine.

Actually, let me reconsider the 1D convex problem solution more cleverly. new_max(t) = max_k (c[k] + t D[k]) [ignoring the tiny -2t² cross], convex PL in t. The minimizer t* over feasible interval [t_lo, t_hi] is where the subgradient contains 0. The subgradient of max_k(a_k + t b_k) at t is the convex hull of {b_k : k argmax}. So the function's slope is b of the current max-k as t increases, and slope is nondecreasing (convex). So the minimizer is where the max-k's b changes sign. Specifically, as t goes from t_lo to t_hi, the active k (argmax) changes. The optimal t is the largest t where the current-active slope ≤ 0 (then at that kink slope jumps above 0).

def local_search(h, n, c, dx, deadline, use_golden=False, golden_evals=18, max_passes=...):
    # h in [0,1], sum n/2, c = correlate(h,1-h,'full'), dx=2/n
    L = n-1
    ti = np.arange(-(n-1), n)  # length 2n-1, t=idx-L
    # precompute for speed: we'll build A_i on the fly
    best_cmax = c.max()
    # operator sequence with several step sizes
    steps = ...
    for step in steps:
       for pass in range(passes):
          improved=False
          for i in range(n):
             for j in range(i+1, n):
                # candidate +step at i, -step at j
                di = step; dj = -step
                if 0<=h[i]+di and h[i]+di<=1 and 0<=h[j]+dj and h[j]+dj<=1:
                    new_c = c + di*Ai - dj*Aj ... 
```

Smarter per-candidate O(1): Precompute for the current c and A (all of them) an array; then a candidate move's new_c = c + di·Ai - dj·Aj - 2d²e_L. To get new_c.max() in O(1) is impossible in general (need max over n entries). BUT, note that only a small number of c entries are near the max (the "active" peaks). Typically only a handful (say ≤ 20) of shifts are within a small window of the max. If I precompute the active set S = {k : c[k] > c.max() - tol} each pass (tol small), then new_c.max() over all k ≈ max( max_{k∈S} (c[k]+Δ[k]), max_{k∉S}(c[k]+Δ[k]) ). For k∉S, c[k] is already well below max; a small move won't lift them above the max, EXCEPT if the move strongly affects them. But the move only changes the max if it reduces the current max peaks; the new max is likely still within S or just below. So approximating new_max ≈ max_{k∈S}(c[k]+Δ[k]) is usually fine for DESCENT (we only accept if the true new max < current max). To be safe, I can compute the true max only for ACCEPT candidates (when the S-approx says it improves), and verify with a full c.max() before accepting. Since S is small (~10-30), per-candidate cost is O(|S|) for the check, O(n) only when actually updating c (rare, per accepted move). Let me do that:

- Each pass: compute S = indices where c[k] ≥ cmax - window (window ~ 5e-3·dx? or a few). Actually many shifts could be near max. Let me set window a bit bigger to be safe, and use S size capped. But if S is large (~50), O(50) per candidate × 20000 = 1e6 per pass, fine.

Wait, but new_c at k for k not in S could exceed cmax after the move if the move ADDS to k. But the move is chosen to REDUCE the max peaks (it's a descent candidate). If a candidate REDUCES all of S but the move ADDS to some non-S k, the true new max might be that non-S k. But we only accept if true new max < old cmax. If the move reduces S and might add to non-S k, the non-S k before was < cmax - window; after adding Δ[k] (bounded by Δmax ≈ 2·d·something small), it might exceed cmax only if Δ[k] > window. For small d, Δ[k] is small (O(d·n)), so unlikely to jump a full window. So the S-approx is safe-ish for small steps. I'll use small steps in operator phase. And verify on accept with full max (cheap: one c.max() O(n) per accept, and accepts are limited per pass).

Hmm, but the S-approx of new_max might UNDERESTIMATE if the new max is a non-S peak, leading us to accept a move that actually doesn't reduce (or increases) the true max. To compute gradient of the smooth max: I need the argmax weights p_k = softmax(α f_k) truncated to a small window (since α large → p concentrated on the max). Then ∇F_α = Σ_k p_k ∇f_k. = Σ_p [δ_{p+t,i}(1-h[p]) - h[p+t] δ_{p,i}]

That's the same A_i[idx] (linear) I had. The -2h[i]? The second derivative has that but first derivative is what matters for gradient. Let me write operator_sequence(h, n, deadline):
```
c = np.correlate(h,1-h,'full'); dx=2/n; L=n-1
ti = np.arange(-(n-1), n)
best = c.max()
def Ai(i): # returns vector A_i (length 2n-1), the per-unit delta for h[i]
   idx_q = i - ti   # i - t
   idx_h = i + ti   # i + t
   A = np.zeros(2*n-1)
   vq = (idx_q>=0)&(idx_q<n); A[vq] += (1-h[idx_q[vq]])
   vh = (idx_h>=0)&(idx_h<n); A[vh] -= h[idx_h[vh]]
   return A
```
Wait careful: A_i[idx] = (1-h[i-t])·1{i-t valid} - h[i+t]·1{i+t valid}, where t=ti[idx]. So for the array A, at position idx, the t is ti[idx]. So A[idx] = (1 - h[i - ti[idx]]) - h[i + ti[idx]], with validity. Let me implement:
```
def make_A(i, h, ti, n):
    nxt = i - ti   # index for (1-h) term: i - t
    phx = i + ti   # index for -h term: i + t
    A = np.zeros(2*n-1)
    m1 = (nxt>=0)&(nxt<n); A[m1] += (1.0 - h[nxt[m1]])
    m2 = (phx>=0)&(phx<n); A[m2] -= h[phx[m2]]
    return A
```
Check indices: (1-h[i-t]) → i-t = i - ti[idx] = nxt[idx]. ✓. h[i+t] → phx[idx]. ✓.

Then candidate move (i: +d, j: -d): new_c = c + d·A_i - d·A_j; new_c[L] -= 2*d*d. new_max = new_c.max(). Let me now write the projection:
```
def project_feasible(h, n):
    target = n/2.0
    h = np.clip(h,0,1)
    # adjust sum to target by adding proportional where possible
    for _ in range(1000):
        cur = h.sum()
        diff = target - cur
        if abs(diff) < 1e-12: break
        if diff > 0:
            room = 1-h
            tot = room.sum()
            if tot <= 1e-15: break
            h = h + (diff/tot)*room   # this may overshoot if some hit 1, but room>0 only for <1
            h = np.clip(h,0,1)
        else:
            tot = h.sum()
            if tot <=1e-15: break
            h = h - ( -diff/tot)*h   # reduce proportionally: h*(1 + diff/tot), diff<0 → factor<1
            h = np.clip(h,0,1)
    # final: might be off by tiny; do a last proportional exact
    # adjust the largest gap
    diff = target - h.sum()
    if abs(diff)>1e-12:
        # add diff to the element with max room if needed
        ...
    return h
```
The two-phase proportional (add where room, remove where value) usually converges exactly. I'll then nudge one element to make sum exact (to float) as a fallback. Actually the continuous task checks ∫h=1 probably within tol; and the requirement "sum(h) == n/2 exactly" — I'll make sum(h) as close to n/2 as possible (within 1e-9) which is effectively exact for float. Let me add a final nudge: find where to add residual.

Let me make robust: after loop, diff = target - sum(h). If |diff|>0: if diff>0 add to the index with largest room (1-h) but not exceeding; else reduce from an index with value>0. This ensures exactly target (within float). I'll implement.

Now SLSQP objective with analytic grad:
```
def smooth_max_and_grad(f, alpha, cmax):
    # f length 2n-1
    w = f - cmax
    # only keep w > -K/alpha
    keep = w > -(60.0/alpha)
    if not keep.any():
        # fallback
        ...
    ps = np.zeros_like(f)
    exps = np.exp(alpha * w[keep] - alpha*0)  # since cmax shift, w[keep] <=0
    # normalize
    s = exps.sum()
    p = np.zeros_like(f)
    p[keep] = exps/s
    smax = cmax + np.log(s)/alpha
    return smax, p
```
Wait smooth max = cmax + (1/α) log(Σ_k exp(α(f_k - cmax))). Since f_k - cmax ≤0, exp ≤1, sum ≤ 2n-1. Good, no overflow (exps ≤1). Keep only those with f_k - cmax > -K/α (exp below e^{-K} negligible). K=30 or 50.

grad: g = Σ_k p_k ∇f_k. ∇f_k = dx·A(k) where A is vector (length n): A_k[i] = (1-h[i-t_k])1{valid} - h[i+t_k]1{valid}, t_k = idx_k - L.
Build for active idx's:
```
g = np.zeros(n)
dx = 2.0/n
L = n-1
for idx in active_idxs:
    t = idx - L
    # V[i] = (1-h[i-t])1{i-t valid} - h[i+t]1{i+t valid}
    V = build_V(t, h)   # O(n)
    g += p[idx]*dx*V
```
build_V(t,h): 
```
def build_V(t, h):
    n = len(h)
    V = np.zeros(n)
    # term1: (1-h[i-t]) for i-t in [0,n-1] → i in [t, t+n-1] ∩ [0,n-1]
    # term2: -h[i+t] for i+t in [0,n-1] → i in [-t, -t+n-1] ∩ [0,n-1]
    a = max(0, t); b = min(n, t+n)
    # term1: for i in [a,b): V[i] += (1-h[i-t])
    V[a:b] += (1-h[t:b-t])   # i-t ranges t.. b-t -1? i from a to b-1, i-t from a-t to b-1-t
    # a-t = max(0,t)-t; if t>=0 → 0; if t<0 → -2t? wait a = max(0,t). a-T:
    hmm let me just do with explicit index arrays for clarity (slower but clear); n ≤ 480 so okay.
```
Let me just implement build_V with explicit index arrays for correctness:
```
def build_V(t, h):
    n = len(h)
    i = np.arange(n)
    iq = i - t   # i - t
    ih = i + t   # i + t
    V = np.zeros(n)
    mq = (iq>=0)&(iq<n); V[mq] += (1.0 - h[iq[mq]])
    mh = (ih>=0)&(ih<n); V[mh] -= h[ih[mh]]
    return V
```
This is O(n) per active idx with a couple numpy ops. For ~ (2n) active worst case but with α large active is small. Even if active ~ 40, that's 40 O(n) builds, fine.

Wait, earlier I had two conventions for t mapping to idx: A_i[idx] = (1-h[i-t]) - h[i+t] where t=idx-L (that's the conv N I derived as A_i[idx]= q[i-t]-h[i+t]). And here V[i] for active idx uses same t=idx-L. Consistent with the gradient I derived (∂c_full[idx]/∂h[i] = A_i[idx] = (1-h[i-t])-h[i+t]). Let me recheck: earlier gradient derivation gave ∂c_full[idx]/∂h[i] = (1-h[i-t])1{i-t valid} - h[i+t]1{i+t valid}. Yes matches V[i]. Good. And this A_i is used in the operator-sequence too for Δc_full[idx] from h[i]+δ = δ·A_i[idx] (plus -δ² at idx=L for the self term, which is second order, and for the gradient it's not included (gradient uses linear)). For the operator sequence finite move, I included -2d² at L. Good.

So build_A(i) for operator = the vector over idx of A_i[idx] = (1-h[i-t_idx]) - h[i+t_idx], t_idx = idx-L. Let me make it consistent with build_V: Note A_i (as vector over idx) at position idx uses t=idx-L: A_i[idx] = (1-h[i - (idx-L)]) - h[i + (idx-L)]. Whereas build_V(t)[i] = (1-h[i-t]) - h[i+t]. So A_i (over idx) is build_V evaluated with the role swapped: A_i[idx] = (1-h[(i) - t_idx]) where the "i" in build_V is our i and t=t_idx. Actually A_i[idx] = (1-h[i - t_idx]) - h[i + t_idx] = build_V(t_idx)[i] (with build_V's "i" = our i, t=t_idx). Wait build_V(t)[i] = (1-h[i-t]) - h[i+t]. Set t = t_idx: build_V(t_idx)[i] = (1-h[i - t_idx]) - h[i + t_idx] = A_i[idx]. Yes! So A_i (vector over idx) has entries A_i[idx] = build_V(t_idx=idx-L)[i]. But build_V returns a vector over "i" for fixed t. So A_i is the transpose-ish. To build A_i (vector indexed by idx), I can loop idx... or note A_i[idx] as function of idx: let u = idx-L = t. A_i idx corresponds to t = u: A = build over u of (1-h[i-u]) - h[i+u]. So A_i = [for u in -(L)..L: (1-h[i-u]) - h[i+u]] (validity gated). So:
```
def build_A_i(i, h, ti, n):  # ti = np.arange(-(n-1), n), length 2n-1 = L*2L+... length = 2L+1 = 2n-1
    u = ti
    iq = i - u   # i - u
    ih = i + u   # i + u
    A = np.zeros(len(ti))
    mq = (iq>=0)&(iq<n); A[mq] += (1.0 - h[iq[mq]])
    mh = (ih>=0)&(ih<n); A[mh] -= h[ih[mh]]
    return A
```
Yes, that's make_A earlier. Good, consistent with V.

But careful: in build_A_i, the "i" here is the index of the VARIABLE we're changing, and we build over u (=t=idx-L). And V[i] for build_V(t) is (1-h[i-t]) - h[i+t] — same formula with roles: here index of variable i vs. shift u. In build_A_i we fix variable index i and vary shift u → A[for idx=u+L]. In build_V we fix shift t and vary variable index i → V[for variable i]. They're transposes of the same bilinear relation: M[var, idx] where M[var, idx (t=idx-L)] = (1-h[var - t]) - h[var + t]. build_A_i(i) = M[i, :] (row i). build_V(t) = M[:, t] (column for that t). Consistent. Good.

So for the operator sequence, Δc (vector over idx) from h[i]+δ, h[j]-δ = δ*M[i,:] - δ*M[j,:] - 2δ² e_L. = δ A_i - δ A_j - 2δ² e_L where A_i=M[i,:], A_j=M[j,:]. Good.
For SLSQP grad (vector over var): g = Σ_idx p_idx dx * M[:, idx] = Σ_idx p_idx dx * (build_V(idx-L)). Good, I'll compute grad = dx * sum over active idx of p_idx * build_V(idx-L).

Great, consistent.

Now let me make sure I don't confuse M's validity: M[var, t] = (1-h[var-t]) if 0≤var-t<n else 0, minus h[var+t] if 0≤var+t<n else 0. In build_A_i(i), iq = i - u (that's var=i, var-t=i-u ✓), ih = i + u (var+t ✓). In build_V(t), iq = i - t (var-t ✓ with var=i), ih=i+t. Consistent.

Let me write build_V for gradient:
```
def build_V(t, h):
    n = h.shape[0]
    i = np.arange(n)
    iq = i - t
    ih = i + t
    V = np.zeros(n)
    mq = (iq >= 0) & (iq < n)
    V[mq] += (1.0 - h[iq[mq]])
    mh = (ih >= 0) & (ih < n)
    V[mh] -= h[ih[mh]]
    return V
```
And grad:
```
def smooth_max_jac(h, n, alpha):
    q = 1.0 - h
    c = np.correlate(h, q, 'full')
    dx = 2.0 / n
    f = c * dx
    cmax = f.max()
    w = f - cmax
    L = n - 1
    idxs = np.arange(2*n - 1)
    active = np.where(w > (-40.0 / alpha))[0]
    if active.size == 0:
        active = np.array([int(np.argmax(w))])
    ex = np.exp(alpha * w[active])
    p = ex / ex.sum()
    g = np.zeros(n)
    for kk in range(active.size):
        idx = int(active[kk])
        t = idx - L
        V = build_V(t, h)
        g += p[kk] * dx * V
    return g
```
and obj:
```
def smooth_max_obj(h, n, alpha):
    q = 1.0 - h
    c = np.correlate(h, q, 'full')
    dx = 2.0 / n
    f = c * dx
    cmax = float(f.max())
    w = f - cmax
    keep = w > (-40.0 / alpha)
    if not keep.any():
        return cmax + 1e-3
    ex = np.exp(alpha * w[keep])
    return cmax + float(np.log(ex.sum()) / alpha)
```
Note for SLSQP, fun and jac each recompute c — two correlate calls per SLSQP iteration step (fun+grad). For n=200, np.correlate(200) ~ 40k ops, trivial. Good.

Projected gradient onto {h: 0≤h≤1, ∑h=target}: 
proj(y): clip to [0,1] then adjust to hit target via the water-filling (add where room / remove where value) — same as project_h but from y (which handles sum). Actually project_h(y) does exactly clip+target. So the projection onto the feasible set is project_h. But wait, project as defined (clip then add-to-room/remove-proportional) is NOT the Euclidean projection, it's a feasibility fix. For a simple projected gradient step h ← project( h - lr * g ), using a non-Euclidean "projection" (the add-to-room) isn't standard and might not descend consistently. But as a heuristic it usually works (it's like adjusting along the feasible affine space). Hmm, adding to room (only where <1) is not Euclidean projection (that would be: distribute uniformly the residual across all not-saturated). Let me make project the Euclidean projection + hence the feasibility:
Eucl proj of y onto {0≤h≤1, ∑h=target}: find τ such that ∑ clip(y - τ, 0, 1) = target (binary search over τ), then h=clip(y-τ,0,1). This is the true L2 projection. Let me implement a proper one for the projected gradient method. This gives a clean descent.

Let me implement:
```
def proj_feas(e, n):
    # e in R^n, project to {0<=h<=1, sum=h_n/2}
    target = n/2.0
    lo, hi = float(e.min()) - 2.0, float(e.max()) + 2.0
    # want sum(clip(e - t,0,1)) = target, decreasing in t
    for _ in range(80):
        t = 0.5*(lo+hi)
        s = np.clip(e - t, 0.0, 1.0).sum()
        if s > target:
            hi... 
        # s decreases as t increases
        if s > target:
            lo = t
        else:
            hi = t
    t = 0.5*(lo+hi)
    h = np.clip(e - t, 0.0, 1.0)
    # fix tiny residual
    diff = target - h.sum()
    if diff > 0:
        room = 1-h
        idx = np.where(room>1e-12)[0]
        if idx.size: h[idx[0]] += min(diff, room[idx[0]]) ... 
    else:
        ...
    return
```
This is ~O(n log). Good. This is the Euclidean projection.

Then projected gradient: h ← proj(h - lr * g_smooth). But smooth max grad g approximates ∇ of F_α. Step size via backtracking line search: reduce until F(h_new) ≤ F(h) + c·g·(h_new-h). Since F is not convex, line search ensures descent each step. This is like a gradient method with exact line search (backtracking). Could work and is simple+vectorized (each step O(n·active) grad + O(n²) F eval for line... F eval via correlate O(n²)). With backtracking ~ a few evals per step. For n=200, each step ~ 5 × (grad O(n·active)+ obj O(n²)) → cheap. Do many steps.

Let me design projected gradient with backtracking:
```
def proj_grad(h, n, deadline, alpha_start, alpha_end, iters_target):
    h = proj_feas(h, n)
    best = None
    # we minimize F_alpha = smooth max; but we want the TRUE max minimized. Use anneal alpha up.
    a = alpha_start
    c5 = compute_c5(h,n)
    # We'll do many steps; each step use current alpha.
    lam = 0.5  # initial step
    for it in range(iters_target):
        if time.time()>deadline: break
        g = smooth_max_jac2(h,n,a)   # analytic grad of F_a
        F = smooth_max_obj(h,n,a)
        hm = h.copy()
        acc = False
        for ls in range(30):
            e = hm - lam*g
            hnew = proj_feas(e, n)
            Fnew = smooth_max_obj(hnew,n,a)
            if Fnew <= F + 0.01 * (g @ (hnew - hm)) + 1e-15:
                # accept
                acc = True
                c5new = compute_c5(hnew,n)
                h = hnew; c5 = c5new
                break
            lam *= 0.7
        if not acc:
            # no acceptable step; maybe alpha too high causing noisy; break this alpha stage
            break
        # occasionally increase alpha
        if it % 40 == 0:
            a *= 1.3
            a = min(a, alpha_end)
        lam = min(lam, 1.0) or reset up
    ...
```

def optimize_one(h0, n, t_start, alloc, tag):
    h = project_h(h0, n).copy()
    c5 = compute_c5(h, n)
    # SLSQP stages
    alphas = [800, 2000, 5000, 12000, 30000]
    budget per call ...
    for a in alphas:
        if time.time() > t_start + alloc*0.25: break
        h = slsqp_stage(h, n, a, deadline_call)
        c5 = compute; if improved keep else still keep h (smooth-max-driven moves may still help later)
    # operator-sequence polish (exact max)

Let me define op_sequence with its own inner time checks and a step schedule, computing improvement with the 2D candidate and the -2d² term, using build_A_i for M rows.

Let me write build_A_i to be fast:
```
def build_A_i(i, h, ti):
    n = h.shape[0]
    u = ti
    iq = i - u; ih = i + u
    A = np.zeros(ti.shape[0], dtype=float)
    mq = (iq>=0)&(iq<n)
    A[mq] += (1.0 - h[iq[mq]])
    mh = (ih>=0)&(ih<n)
    A[mh] -= h[ih[mh]]
    return A
```
ti = np.arange(-(n-1), n). Precompute once per op_sequence and reuse. Good.

op_sequence:
```
def op_sequence(h, n, deadline, steps=(0.5,0.2,0.1,0.05,0.02,0.01,0.005,0.002,0.001,0.0005), max_pass_total=200):
    h = h.copy()
    c = np.correlate(h, 1.0-h, 'full'); dx=2.0/n; L=n-1
    ti = np.arange(-(n-1), n)
    cmax = c.max(); best = cmax
    eL = np.zeros(2*n-1); eL[L]=1.0
    passes=0
    for d in steps:
        improved_any=True
        while improved_any and passes<max_pass_total and time.time()<deadline:
            improved_any=False; passes+=1
            for i in range(n):
                if time.time()>deadline: return project_h(h,n), compute_c5(h,n)
                for j in range(i+1, n):
                    # candidate: h[i]+d, h[j]-d
                    if h[i]+d<=1.0 and h[j]-d>=0.0:
                        Ai = build_A_i(i,h,ti); Aj = build_A_i(j,h,ti)
                        new_c = c + d*Ai - d*Aj
                        new_c[L] -= 2.0*d*d
                        nm = new_c.max()
                        if nm < best - 1e-14:
                            c = new_c; h[i]+=d; h[j]-=d; best=nm; improved_any=True
                    # candidate: h[i]-d, h[j]+d
                    if h[i]-d>=0.0 and h[j]+d<=1.0:
                        Ai = build_A_i(i,h,ti); Aj = build_A_i(j,h,ti)
                        new_c = c - d*Ai + d*Aj
                        new_c[L] -= 2.0*d*d
                        nm = new_c.max()
                        if nm < best - 1e-14:
                            c = new_c; h[i]-=d; h[j]+=d; best=nm; improved_any=True
    # final recompute
    h = project_h(h, n)
    c5 = compute_c5(h, n)
    return h, c5
```
Wait, in the inner, I build Ai and Aj separately for the two directions, but they're the same Ai, Aj (the rows don't depend on the direction sign). def optimize_node(h0, n, tdeadline, do_op=True, op_weight=1.0):
   h=project_h(h0,n)
   cs = compute_c5(h,n)
   # SLSQP
   alphas=[600,1600,4000,10000,26000]
   percall = ...
   for a in alphas:
      if time.time()>tdeadline*0.35 or time.time()>tdeadline-3: break
      cd = min(time.time()+ (percall estimate), tdeadline*0.4)
      h,cs = slsqp_stage(h,n,a, cd)
   # op
   if do_op:
      d2 = tdeadline*0.75 (op deadline)
      h,cs = op_sequence(h,n,d2, steps=...)
   # golden
   h,cs = golden(h,n, tdeadline*0.9)
   return project_h(h,n), compute_c5(h,n), len(h)

Now the golden polish:
```
def golden_polish(h, n, deadline, rounds=3):
    h=project_h(h,n).copy(); c=np.correlate(h,1-h,'full'); dx=2/n; L=n-1; ti=np.arange(-(n-1),n)
    invphi=(np.sqrt(5)-1)/2; Phi=1+invphi
    eL=np.zeros(2*n-1); eL[L]=1
    def phiv(t):
        # returns max(c + t*(Ai-Aj) with -2t^2 at L) for the FIXED current pair? no, per call we pass precomputed D
        pass
    for _ in range(rounds):
        if time.time()>deadline: break
        improved=False
        for i in range(n):
            if time.time()>deadline: break
            for j in range(i+1,n):
                if time.time()>deadline: break
                lo=max(-h[i], h[j]-1.0); hi=min(1-h[i], h[j])
                if hi-lo < 1e-9: continue
                Ai=build_A_i(i,h,ti); Aj=build_A_i(j,h,ti); D=Ai-Aj
                # two-point golden
                a2=lo+invphi*(hi-lo); b2=hi-invphi*(hi-lo)
                fa=newmax(c,D,a2); fb=newmax(c,D,b2)
                for _g in range(14):
                    if fa<fb:
                        hi=b2; b2=a2; fb=fa
                        a2=lo+invphi*(hi-lo); fa=newmax(c,D,a2)
                    else:
                        lo=a2; a2=b2; fa=fb
                        b2=hi-invphi*(hi-lo); fb=newmax(c,D,b2)
                t=0.5*(lo+hi)
                ft=newmax(c,D,t)
                if ft < c.max()-1e-13:
                    # apply: h[i]+=t,h[j]-=t
                    h[i]+=t; h[j]-=t
                    c = c + t*(Ai-Aj); c[L]-=2*t*t
                    improved=True
        if not improved: break
    h=project_h(h,n); c5=compute_c5(h,n)
    return h,c5
```
newmax(c,D,t) = max(c + t*D) with the -2t² at L: 
```
def newmax(c,D,t):
    e=c + t*D
    e[L] = e[L] - 2.0*t*t   # but L depends on n; pass L
    return float(e.max())
```

def _validate_update(h,n):
    rng=np.random.RandomState(123)
    c0=np.correlate(h,1.0-h,'full'); L=n-1; ti=np.arange(-(n-1),n)
    ok=True
    for _ in range(8):
        i=int(rng.randint(n)); j=int(rng.randint(n)); d=float(rng.uniform(-0.3,0.3))
        h2=h.copy(); h2[i]+=d; h2[j]-=d; h2=np.clip(h2,0,1)
        # but clip changes effect; for validation use unclipped but ensure within [0,1]
        # choose small d so no clip
        d=float(rng.uniform(-0.05,0.05)); h2=h.copy(); h2[i]+=d; h2[j]-=d
        Ai=build_A_i(i,h,ti); Aj=build_A_i(j,h,ti)
        pred=c0 + d*Ai - d*Aj; pred[L]-=2*d*d
        actual=np.correlate(h2,1.0-h2,'full')
        if not np.allclose(pred,actual,rtol=1e-9,atol=1e-9):
            ok=False; break
        c0=np.clip? no, c0 for next? we update h back (since we use same h base)
    return ok
```

Let me write helper `interp_resample(h0, n0, n1)`:
```
def interp_resample(h0, n0, n1):
    if n0==n1: return h0.copy()
    x0=(np.arange(n0)+0.5)*(2.0/n0)
    x1=(np.arange(n1)+0.5)*(2.0/n1)
    return np.interp(x1, x0, h0)
```

project_h (feasibility with exact sum):
```
def project_h(h, n):
    target=n/2.0
    h=np.clip(np.asarray(h,dtype=float),0.0,1.0)
    for _ in range(2000):
        diff=target-h.sum()
        if abs(diff)<1e-12: break
        if diff>0:
            room=1.0-h; tot=room.sum()
            if tot<=1e-15: break
            h=np.clip(h+(diff/tot)*room,0.0,1.0)
        else:
            tot=h.sum()
            if tot<=1e-15: break
            h=np.clip(h*(1.0+diff/tot),0.0,1.0)
    # final exact nudge on one element
    diff=target-h.sum()
    if diff>1e-12:
        idx=int(np.argmax(1.0-h)); add=min(diff,1.0-h[idx]); h[idx]+=add
    elif diff<-1e-12:
        idx=int(np.argmax(h)); sub=min(-diff,h[idx]); h[idx]-=sub
    return h
```
Good enough.

compute_c5:
```
def compute_c5(h,n):
    return float(np.max(np.correlate(h,1.0-h,'full'))*(2.0/len(h)))
```
Wait use len(h)=n. I'll use n.

slsqp_stage: 
```
def slsqp_stage(h,n,alpha,tdl, iters):
    try:
        cons=[{'type':'eq','fun':csum_fun,'jac':csum_jac,'args':(n,)}]
        bounds=[(0.0,1.0)]*n
        res=minimize(smax_fun,h,args=(n,alpha),method='SLSQP',jac=smax_grad,
                     bounds=bounds,constraints=cons,
                     options={'maxiter':iters,'ftol':1e-12})
        if res.success or hasattr(res,'x') and res.x is not None:
            hx=project_h(res.x,n)
            return hx,compute_c5(hx,n)
    except Exception:
        pass
    return h,compute_c5(h,n)
```
csum_fun(x,n)=float(x.sum()-n/2.0); csum_jac(x,n)=np.ones(len(x)).
smax_fun(x,n,alpha): compute c, f=c*dx, cmax, logsumexp window, return.
smax_grad(x,n,alpha): as above.

def newmax_from_D(c,d,D,L):
    e=c + d*D
    e[L]=e[L]-2.0*d*d
    return float(e.max())

Steps list function:
```
def step_list(n):
    base=0.25
    seq=[]
    s=base
    while s>1e-4:
        seq.append(round(s,6))
        s*=0.5
    seq+= [5e-4,2e-4,1e-4]
    return seq
```

Actually, I realize I can reduce allocations by reusing a scratch D array. Since D=Ai-Aj (I only need D and the two feasibility checks use Ai, Aj individually for the -/+ feasibility? No—feasibility only needs h[i],h[j] values (scalars). The candidate nm uses D=Ai-Aj. So I only need D (one array) per pair, plus I don't even need Ai, Aj separately (just D)! Because new_c = c + d*Ai - d*Aj = c + d*(Ai-Aj) = c + d*D. And the -2d² at L. So I only need to build D = Ai - Aj. Building Ai and Aj separately then subtracting = 2 builds + 1 subtract. Alternatively build D directly: D[idx] = A_i[idx] - A_j[idx] = [(1-h[i-t]) - (1-h[j-t])] - [h[i+t]-h[j+t]] = (h[j-t]-h[i-t]) - (h[i+t]-h[j+t]) = (h[j-t]-h[i+t]) ... let me just compute D directly with two index sets:
D[idx] = (1-h[i-t]) - h[i+t] - (1-h[j-t]) + h[j+t]
       = (h[j-t] - h[i-t]) - (h[i+t] - h[j+t])  [the 1's cancel]
       with validity gating per term.
```
def make_D(i,j,h,ti):
    n=h.shape[0]; u=ti; D=np.zeros(ti.shape[0])
    # term A: h[j-t] where j-t valid
    it_j = j - u; 
    m=(it_j>=0)&(it_j<n); D[m]+=h[it_j[m]]
    # term B: -h[i-t]
    it_i = i - u
    m=(it_i>=0)&(it_i<n); D[m]-=h[it_i[m]]
    # term C: -h[i+t]
    ip_i = i + u
    m=(ip_i>=0)&(ip_i<n); D[m]-=h[ip_i[m]]
    # term D: +h[j+t]
    ip_j = j + u
    m=(ip_j>=0)&(ip_j<n); D[m]+=h[ip_j[m]]
    return D
```
That's 4 masked index ops per pair → ~4 O(n) but one result array. vs 2 builds (make_A_i twice, ~4 ops each =8) +1 subtract. So make_D = 4 ops, better. Let me use make_D. Verify D = A_i - A_j: A_i = (1-h[i-t]) - h[i+t]; A_j = (1-h[j-t]) - h[j+t]. A_i - A_j = (1-h[i-t]) - h[i+t] - (1-h[j-t]) + h[j+t] = (h[j-t]-h[i-t]) - (h[i+t]-h[j+t]) . My make_D: +h[j-t] - h[i-t] - h[i+t] + h[j+t] = same. ✓.

So op_sequence per pair: make_D (4 ops), then candidate nm: e=c + d*D; e[L]-=2d²; nm=e.max() → 3 ops. For the second (opposite) direction: e=c - d*D; e[L]-=2d²; nm → 3 ops. Total ~10 O(n) per pair? 4 (D) + 3 + 3 = 10. Hmm a bit more than earlier estimate. For 20000 pairs ×10×400 = 8e7 element ops/pass → ~0.1s C, plus alloc overhead maybe 0.2-0.3s/pass. OK.

Let me reduce to applying the BEST improving j per i per sub-sweep, and do several sub-sweeps per pass. I'll structure:
```
def op_sequence2(h, n, deadline, steps, maxsub):
    M = build_M(h)  # (n, 2n-1)
    c = np.correlate(h,1-h,'full'); dx=2/n; L=n-1; best=c.max()
    ivcat = True
    for d in steps:
        improved_any=True
        subs=0
        while improved_any and subs<maxsub and time and ...:
            improved_any=False; subs+=1
            for i in range(n):
                # vectorized find best improving j for move +d at i, -d at j; and opposite
                jv = np.arange(i+1,n)
                if jv.size==0: continue
                D = M[i] - M[jv]   # (J, 2n-1)
                # +d direction: feasible j where h[i]+d<=1 and h[j]-d>=0
                feasP = (h[jv] >= d) & (h[i]+d <= 1.0)
                feasN = (h[jv] <= 1.0-d) & (h[i]-d >= 0.0)
                nz = feasP | feasN
                if not nz.any(): continue
                # compute nm for +d and -d
                cL = best # baseline
                # new_c for a candidate j: c + d*(M[i][v])) ... D rows
                # We need max over idx of (c + d*row_j) with row_j = M[i]-M[jv]
                # = c + d*(M[i]) - d*(M[jv]) ; row-based
                # Vectorized: BC = c[None,:] + d*(M[i][None,:] - M[jv]) -> (J,2n-1)
                BC = c[None,:] + d*(M[i][None,:] - M[jv])
                BC[:, L] -= 2*d*d
                nmP = BC.max(axis=1)
                # -d: h[i]-=d, h[jv]+=d => c - d*(M[i]-M[jv])? new_c = c - d*(M[i]) + d*(M[jv]) = c + (-d)*D
                BC2 = c[None,:] - d*(M[i][None,:] - M[jv])  # = c - d*D
                BC2[:, L] -= 2*d*d
                nmN = BC2.max(axis=1)
                # For each jv, best of the two directions (but a +d accepted is h[i]+d,h[j]-d; -d is h[i]-d,h[j]+d)
                # choose improvement = min over directions of nm, respecting feasibility
                nm = np.maximum(nmP, 1e9)  # default infeasible
                nm = np.where(feasP, nmP, nm)
                nm = np.where(feasN, np.minimum(nm, nmN), nm)
                # pick best jv with nm < best
                ...
```
This is O(n²) memory (J×(2n-1)) per i per sub-sweep. For n=400, J up to 399, (399×799)=3.2e5 floats ~2.5MB per i (transient, freed each i). Fine.

But this allocates big arrays per i per sub-sweep → 400 i × few subs × (array alloc ~2.5MB) → heavy transient allocation, but C handles. For n=400 maybe okay for a few sub-sweeps; I'll cap maxsub for big n.

Then pick best jv index with nm<best, determine direction (compare nmP vs nmN for that j), apply: h[i]±d, h[j]∓d; update M rows (O(n)), update c (O(n) using M rows). Then continue (the other jv in this i's list are stale after accept; so I break out of this i's vector selection and re-scan i after next i? or just do one accept per i per sub-sweep then move to next i). To keep M consistent within building BC for the SAME i, I haven't accepted yet, so BC valid. After accepting, M changes; other jv in this i's list would be stale, but since I only accept one per i per sub-sweep, that's fine (next sub-sweep re-scans). So one accept per i per sub-sweep → per sub-sweep up to n accepts. Convergence needs ~ O(n) sub-sweeps? Possibly. Hmm, one accept per i per sub-sweep × n i = n accepts/sub-sweep; to fully descend maybe fewer sub-sweeps. Let me do per i: pick top k=1 best j (no, just best) → one accept. Then subs until no accept. Number of subs maybe 10-50. × n i × O(n²) 2D per i per sub → O(subs × n × n²) = O(subs × n³). For n=400, subs=20, n³=6.4e7 → 20×6.4e7 = 1.28e9 C-ops → maybe 1-3s per node. For a big node. Fine.

I'll set maxsub ~ 40 for coarse, ~ 12 for fine, and rely on time.

Let me step back and consider a possibly simpler yet effective path: Given the tightness (need to shave 2e-5), maybe the FINEST reasonable n with a SOLID local search (even the per-+(i,j) numpy-heavy version but with M maintained) is enough. Let me implement build_M efficiently:
```
def build_M(h):
    n=h.shape[0]; L=n-1; idx=np.arange(2*n-1); t=idx-L
    irows=np.arange(n).astype(float)[:,None]
    im = irows - t[None,:]
    ip = irows + t[None,:]
    M=np.zeros((n,2*n-1))
    m1=(im>=0)&(im<n); M[m1]+= (1.0-h[im.astype(int)[m1]])
    m2=(ip>=0)&(ip<n); M[m2]-= h[ip.astype(int)[m2]]
    return M
```
Careful with fancy indexing: im is float; use int where valid. im.astype(int)[m1] where m1 masks to valid (int conversion of valid range fine). Actually convert: im_i = im.astype(int) but invalid entries become some int; only used where m1 true. Let me do: imI=im.astype(int); then M[m1] += 1-h[imI[m1]]. Works.

M update on accept (h[i]+=di, h[j]+=dj; here di=+d, dj=-d):
For each row p, columns change:
- from h[i] by di: column t = p-i → idx = L+(p-i); value change: M[p,idx] has term (1-h[p-t]) at t=p-i: p-t=p-(p-i)=i → yes that's the (1-h[i]) term. Δ(1-h[i]) with h[i]+=di → Δ = -di. So M[p, L+(p-i)] -= di (if idx valid).
   also term -h[p+t] at t=i-p → idx=L+(i-p): p+t=p+(i-p)=i → Δ(-h[i]) with h[i]+=di → -di. So M[p, L+(i-p)] -= di.
- from h[j] by dj: M[p, L+(p-j)] -= dj; M[p, L+(j-p)] -= dj.
So for all p, M[p, L+(p-i)] -= di, M[p,L+(i-p)] -= di, M[p,L+(p-j)] -= dj, M[p,L+(j-p)] -= dj (with validity 0≤...<2n-1).
That's O(n) rows × 4 cols = 4n scalar updates. I can vectorize over p: 
rows = np.arange(n)
For column c = L+(rows-i): idx_i1 = L+(rows-i) where valid → M[rows_valid, idx_i1] -= di. Similarly others. Let me code:
```
def M_update(M, i, di, j, dj, n):
    L=n-1; rows=np.arange(n)
    # column a1 = L+(rows - i): valid 0<=. <2n-1
    a = L + (rows - i)
    m = (a>=0)&(a<2*n-1)
    M[rows[m], a[m]] -= di
    b = L + (i - rows)
    m = (b>=0)&(b<2*n-1)
    M[rows[m], b[m]] -= di
    c1 = L + (rows - j)
    m = (c1>=0)&(c1<2*n-1)
    M[rows[m], c1[m]] -= dj
    d1 = L + (j - rows)
    m = (d1>=0)&(d1<2*n-1)
    M[rows[m], d1[m]] -= dj
```
Wait sign: for h[i] increase by di, Δ M at (1-h[i]) term is -di (since (1-h) decreases). At -h[i] term is -di (since -h decreases). So both -= di. For h[j] by dj (dj=-d, i.e., h[j] decreases by d): at (1-h[j]): (1-(h[j]+dj)) - (1-h[j]) = -dj → M -= dj (dj negative → M increases). At -h[j] term: -((h[j]+dj)) - (-h[j]) = -dj → M -= dj. So both -= dj. Consistent: M_update as above (M -= di at the two i-columns, M -= dj at the two j-columns). 

But careful: are the columns for i and j possibly overlapping (if i, j close)? If a column equals another (e.g., L+(p-i)=L+(i-p) when p-i = i-p → p=i, which for p=i gives column L; both the (1-h) and -h terms at t=0 → column L twice). Indeed at p=i, a=L+(i-i)=L and b=L+(i-i)=L → same column, and we'd apply -= di twice → total -2di at M[i,L]. Is that correct? M[i,L] = (1-h[i-0]) - h[i+0] = (1-h[i]) - h[i] = 1-2h[i]. Change h[i]+=di → 1-2(h[i]+di) = (1-2h[i]) - 2di → Δ=-2di. ✓ So applying -=di twice gives -2di. Correct! Good, the double-application at p=i (and p=j) gives the right -2di/-2dj at M[i,L]/M[j,L]. And if i and j columns collide further (e.g., L+(p-i)=L+(p-j)? that needs i=j, not the case), fine.

Also M[i, L] and M[j, L] get the self contributions correctly.

Great, M maintained incrementally.

Now c update per accept: c = c + di*M[i] + dj*M[j]? Let me check: c[idx] = Σ_p h[p]·... The change in c from h[i]+=di is di * (∂c/∂h[i]) = di * M[i] (since M[i, idx] = ∂c[idx]/∂h[i] = A_i[idx]). Yes ∂c/∂h[i] = M[i] (the row). Wait earlier M[i, idx] = A_i[idx] = ∂c_full[idx]/∂h[i]. So ∂c/∂h[i] (vector over idx) = M[i]. So c += di*M[i] + dj*M[j]. Plus the second-order -di²- dj² at L (from (h+di)(q-di) cross). Actually the finite change in c at idx from h[i]+di, h[j]+dj = di*M[i][idx] + dj*M[j][idx] - (cross). The cross terms: for idx where BOTH the (1-h[i]) and ... the cross -di² arises at idx=L for i (self), -dj² at L for j. So c[L] -= di² + dj². So:
```
c += di*M[i] + dj*M[j]
c[L] -= di*di + dj*dj
```
Consistent with earlier (di=d, dj=-d → c[L] -= d² + d² = -2d², di*M[i]+dj*M[j] = d M[i] - d M[j]). ✓.

Now the vectorized-over-j op_sequence:
```
def op_sequence_vec(h, n, deadline, steps, maxsub_per_step, minpass_noimprove_stop=True):
    h=project_h(h,n).copy()
    L=n-1; dx=2.0/n
    M=build_M(h); c=np.correlate(h,1.0-h,'full'); best=float(c.max())
    for d in steps:
        if time.time()>deadline: break
        subs=0
        improved_any=True
        while improved_any and subs<maxsub_per_step and time.time()<deadline:
            improved_any=False; subs+=1
            for i in range(n):
                if time.time()>deadline: return h, compute_c5(h,n)
                jv=np.arange(i+1,n)
                if jv.size<1: continue
                hbar=h[jv]
                # feasible +d (h[i]+d<=1 and h[jv]-d>=0 => h[jv]>=d)
                feasP = (h[jv] >= d) and h[i]<=1.0-d   # scalar and
                feasN = (h[jv] <= 1.0-d) and h[i]>=0.0-d
                if not ((feasP) or (feasN)): continue
                # BC for all jv rows
                D = M[i][None,:] - M[jv]      # (J, 2n-1)   = M[i]-M[j]
                base = c[None,:] + d*(M[i][None,:] - M[jv])   # +d direction
                base[:,L] -= 2.0*d*d
                nmP = base.max(axis=1)
                base2 = c[None,:] - d*(M[i][None,:] - M[jv])   # -d
                base2[:,L] -= 2.0*d*d
                nmN = base2.max(axis=1)
                nm = np.full(jv.size, np.inf)
                nm = np.where(feasP, nmP, nm)
                nm = np.where(feasN, np.minimum(nm, nmN), nm)
                # best improving j
                order=np.argsort(nm)
                chosen=-1; dirn=0
                for kk in order:
                    jj=int(jv[kk]); val=nm[kk]
                    if val < best-1e-14:
                        chosen=jj; dirn=1 if (feasP[jj-i-1] and (not feasN[jj-i-1] or nmP[jj-i-1]<=nmN[jj-i-1])) else -1
                        break
                if chosen<0: continue
                # apply
                if dirn==1:
                    di=d; dj=-d
                else:
                    di=-d; dj=+d
                ii=i; jj=chosen
                h[ii]+=di; h[jj]+=dj
                M_update(M, ii, di, jj, dj, n)
                c += di*M[ii] + dj*M[jj]
                c[L] -= di*di + dj*dj
                # recompute best as new max(should be min)
                nmval=c.max(); best=nmval; improved_any=True
                # continue to next i (this i's jv now stale)
        # after step d loop
    h=project_h(h,n); return h, compute_c5(h,n)
```
Wait, I compute D but then recompute base using M[i]-M[jv] twice; I can reuse D. Let me set D=M[i][None,:]-M[jv] once; base=c[None,:]+d*D; base2=c[None,:]-d*D. Good.

nmP=base.max(axis=1) is length J. feass are boolean arrays length J. Choose best.

With maxsub maybe 8-12 for n=400 → ~8-10s per step × (maybe 4-6 steps, early ones short) → could be 40-60s per fine node. Hmm a bit much. I'll cap: for n>~320, maxsub small and steps few. ```
def run(seed=42,budget_s=1100,**kw):
  t0=time.time()
  run_dead = t0 + budget_s - 8
  rng=np.random.RandomState(seed)
  # parent
  try:
    pah=np.array(initial_h_values,dtype=float).copy(); pn=len(pah)
  except Exception:
    pah=np.full(200,0.5); pn=200
  pah=project_h(pah,pn)
  bh=pah.copy(); bc=compute_c5(pah,pn); bn=pn
  # node plan
  nodes=[(200,130),(240,105),(270,105),(300,120),(340,130),(380,150)]
  # first node maybe sn not pn? parent is pn(200). start from pah.
  # run nodes, interpolate best->n
  acc=0.0
  start_h=pah; start_n=pn
  for (n,maxrun) in nodes:
     if time.time()>=run_dead-6: break
     node_dead = t0 + something? use min(now+maxrun? , ...)
     # dynamic: allow up to now+maxrun but also not exceed run_dead-70
     nd = min(time.time()+maxrun, run_dead-70)
     h_in = start_h.copy()
     if start_n!=n:
        h_in = interp_resample(start_h, start_n, n)
     topt = optimize_node(h_in, n, nd, rng)
     c5=topt[1]
     if c5 < bc-1e-15:
        bc=c5; bh=topt[0]; bn=n
        start_h = bh.copy(); start_n = bn   # refine further from best
     # else keep start_h as previous best (don't degrade)
  # Additional fine nodes with leftover: 420,460
  for n in [420,460]:
     if time.time()>=run_dead-70: break
     nd=min(time.time()+130, run_dead-70)
     h_in = interp_resample(bh, bn, n) if bn!=n else bh.copy()
     topt=optimize_node(h_in,n,nd,rng, heavy=False)
     if topt[1]<bc-1e-15:
        bc=topt[1]; bh=topt[0]; bn=n
  # restarts on best (and a fine n) with leftover
  if time.time()<run_dead-40:
     # 2-3 restarts at bn
     scales=[0.03,0.06,0.10]
     for s in scales:
        if time.time()>=run_dead-35: break
        hp=bh + rng.normal(0,s,bn); hp=project_h(hp,bn)
        nd=min(time.time()+60, run_dead-35)
        topt=optimize_node(hp,bn,nd,rng, heavy=False)
        if topt[1]<bc-1e-15:
            bc=topt[1]; bh=topt[0]
  # final micro polish at best
  if time.time()<run_dead-8:
     bh2=op_sequence_vec(bh,bn, min(time.time()+35, run_dead-8), steps_micro, maxsub=6)
     recompute; if better set.
  # final exact c5
  bh=project_h(bh,bn); bc=compute_c5(bh,bn)
  return bh, bc, bn
```
optimize_node(h0,n,nd,rng,heavy=True):
```
def optimize_node(h0,n,nd,rng,heavy=True):
   h=project_h(h0,n); c5=compute_c5(h,n)
   cs0=c5
   # SLSQP
   remain_sls = 0.30*(nd - time.time())
   slsqp_dead = time.time()+max(3.0, remain_sls)
   hs,c5s = do_slsqp(h,n,slsqp_dead)
   start = (h,cs0) if cs0<=c5s else (hs,c5s)
   h,s=c5... = start
   # op
   heavycfg by n
   op_dead = min(nd-3, time.time()+ (0.55*(nd-time.time())))
   h,c5 = op_sequence_vec(h,n,op_dead,steps, maxsub)
   # golden if allowed
   if n<=320 and time.time()<nd-3:
        h,c5=golden(h,n,min(nd-1,time.time()+ (nd-time.time())*0.25), rounds by n)
   h=project_h(h,n); c5=compute_c5(h,n)
   return h,c5
```
do_slsqp(h,n,dead): run stages until dead.

      h1,c1 = slsqp_stage(h,n,a,dead,iters)
      h=h1
      if c1<compute_c5(h? ) ... 
   # final project
   h=project_h(h,n); c=compute_c5(h,n)
   return h,c

Let me now write golden using M:
```
def golden_m(h,n,deadline,rounds=2):
   h=project_h(h,n).copy(); M=build_M(h); c=np.correlate(h,1-h,'full'); L=n-1; best=c.max()
   for _ in range(rounds):
      if time.time()>deadline: break
      improved=False
      for i in range(n):
         if time.time()>deadline: break
         jv=np.arange(i+1,n)
         for jj in jv:   # this is per-pair golden (O(n² × iters × n)) heavy; limit by time
             lo=max(-h[i], h[jj]-1.0); hi=min(1-h[i], h[jj])
             if hi-lo<1e-8: continue
             D=M[i]-M[jj]
             # golden over t
             ips=(5**0.5-1)/2.0
             a2=lo+ips*(hi-lo); b2=hi-ips*(hi-lo)
             fa=mm(c,D,a2,L); fb=mm(c,D,b2,L)
             for _g in range(12):
                if fa<fb: hi=b2; b2=a2; fb=fa; a2=lo+ips*(hi-lo); fa=mm(c,D,a2,L)
                else: lo=a2; a2=b2; fa=fb; b2=hi-ips*(hi-lo); fb=mm(c,D,b2,L)
             t=0.5*(lo+hi)
             ft=mm(c,D,t,L)
             if ft < best-1e-13:
                # apply t
                di=t; dj=-t
                h[i]+=di; h[jj]+=dj
                M_update(M,i,di,jj,dj,n)
                c+=di*M[i]+dj*M[jj]; c[L]-=di*di+dj*dj
                best=c.max(); improved=True
                # note: after accept, M[i],M[jj] etc changed for other pairs in this i; but D used was pre-accept only for THIS pair; subsequent jj recompute D fresh. good.
      if not improved: break
   h=project_h(h,n); return h, compute_c5(h,n)
```
mm(c,D,t,L): e=c+t*D; e[L]-=2t²; return e.max().
This golden is O(n² × 12 × O(n)) = O(12 n³)/round — heavy for n~320 (~12×3.3e7=4e8 C ~1s/round). For n≤320 rounds 2-3 → 2-3s. OK but adds up. I'll only run golden when time allows and cap rounds by n; for n>320 skip.

But golden per-pair loop in Python over n² pairs each doing 12 mm (each a couple numpy ops) → n²×12 numpy calls: for n=320, 102400×12=1.2M calls/round → ~ a few seconds overhead. Acceptable for a couple rounds. For n=240, 28800×12=345k calls/round ~ 1s. OK. I'll limit golden to n≤300 to be safe, 2 rounds.

Alright, let me finalize. This is getting long but let me write it.

Let me also make sure build_M output used by M_update uses same indexing (n rows × (2n-1) cols). M_update updates M[rows, col]. Consistent.

Let me now also verify build_M sign/meaning equals ∂c/∂h (rows) which equals A_i: M[i, idx] should equal ∂c_full[idx]/∂h[i]. Earlier we had M[var, idx] = (1-h[var - t]) - h[var + t] (with t=idx-L) [from gradient]. In build_M I set M[i, idx] = (1-h[i - t]) where term1 index = im = irows - t (i - t) ✓, minus h[ip] where ip=irows + t = i + t ✓. So M[i, idx] = (1-h[i-t]) - h[i+t] = ∂c/∂h[i]. ✓ matches M rows = A_i. 

But wait: earlier gradient of smooth max used V_i(idx) = (1-h[i-t]) - h[i+t], and I set g += p*V_i. That V_i is exactly M[i] (row i). So g = (Σ active p_idx) M[i] (over idx) × dx = Σ_idx Pidx × M[i] per i. So g = (P^T? ) M.T? g_i = dx * Σ_idx M[i, idx] P[idx] = dx * (M @ P)[i] where P is the length-(2n-1) weight vector. So g = dx * (M @ P)?? M is (n × (2n-1)); M @ P (P length (2n-1)) → length n. Yes! So I can compute the gradient much faster as g = dx * (M.T? no, M (n×C) @ P → n). Let me verify: g[i] = Σ_idx M[i,idx]*P[idx] → that's (M @ P)[i] for P a 1-D of length C. Yes. So grad = dx * (M @ P). But M depends on h (recompute M each grad call — O(n²) via build_M, which is a 2D op). build_M is O(n²) (a few 2D ops) → cheap. And P = softmax weights over active idx (length C). So smax_grad = dx * (M @ P_active) — a single matrix-vector product! Much faster and simpler than looping active idx. Let me use that (and M is computed from current h inside the grad). 

So smax_grad(x,n,alpha):
```
q=x; M=build_M(x); c=correlate(x,1-x); dx=2/n; f=c*dx; cm=f.max(); w=f-cm; keep=w>-(40/alpha); P=zeros(2n-1); e=np.exp(alpha*w[keep]); P[keep]=e/e.sum(); g=dx*(M@P); return g
```
build_M is O(n²) 2D ops — for n=400: M is 400×799, building ~ few 2D ops ~ fast (~ms). And M@P is (n×C)@(C,)=fast. So grad cheap. And smax_obj recomputes c (O(n²)). So SLSQP with analytic grad is efficient. 

Similarly smax_obj:
```
c=correlate; dx; f=c dx; cm; w; keep; e=exp(alpha w keep); return cm + log(sum e)/alpha
```
Good.

Let me write smax and slsqp_stage:
```
def smax_obj(x,n,alpha):
    c=np.correlate(x,1.0-x,'full'); dx=2.0/n; f=c*dx
    cm=float(f.max()); w=f-cm
    keep=w > (-(40.0/alpha))
    if not keep.any(): return cm
    e=np.exp(alpha*w[keep]); s=e.sum()
    return cm + float(np.log(s)/alpha)

def smax_grad(x,n,alpha):
    M=build_M(x)
    c=np.correlate(x,1.0-x,'full'); dx=2.0/n; f=c*dx
    cm=float(f.max()); w=f-cm
    keep=w > (-(40.0/alpha))
    P=np.zeros(2*n-1)
    if keep.any():
        e=np.exp(alpha*w[keep]); P[keep]=e/e.sum()
    else:
        P[int(np.argmax(w))]=1.0
    g=dx*(M@P)
    return g

def csum_fun(x,n):
    return float(np.sum(x)-0.5*n)
```

Let me finalize slsqp_stage:
```
def slsqp_stage(h,n,alpha,dead,iters):
    try:
        cons=[{'type':'eq','fun':csum_fun,'args':(n,)}]
        bounds=[(0.0,1.0)]*floatn...
        res=minimize(smax_obj, h, args=(n,alpha), method='SLSQP', jac=smax_grad,
                     bounds=bounds, constraints=cons,
                     options={'maxiter':iters,'ftol':1e-11})
        hx=project_h(res.x, n)
        return hx, float(np.max(np.correlate(hx,1.0-hx,'full'))*(2.0/n))
    except Exception:
        return h, float(np.max(np.correlate(h,1.0-h,'full'))*(2.0/n))
```

Now, potential subtle bug: In M_update, both a and b for the i-term are applied -= di, but if a and b refer to the SAME column for some row (p=i → a=L, b=L), we apply -=di twice → -2di at M[i,L] which is correct (self). If a equals some column that's also a j-term column (py coincidence), we'd sum di and dj appropriately (both changes contribute to that cell) — which is correct since both h[i] and h[j] affect that M entry. Good.

Also note: M[i, L] = 1 - 2h[i]. After M_update for i: M[i,L] -= di (from a at p=i) and -= di (from b at p=i) → M[i,L] -= 2di → 1-2(h[i]+di) ✓. And for j: M[j,L] -=2dj → 1-2(h[j]+dj) ✓. And cross terms (M[p,L] for p≠i,j get contributions if a or b = L for that p: a=L+(p-i)=L → p=i; b=L+(i-p)=L→p=i. So only p=i. Similarly j. So M[other, L] unchanged. But should M[other, L] change? M[p,L] = (1-h[p]) - h[p] (t=0) = 1-2h[p], depends only on h[p] → changes only if p is i or j. ✓.

Great, M_update correct.

Now, one more: c update `c += di*M[i] + dj*M[j]` — but after M_update, M[i], M[j] rows have been updated (they now reflect new h). I should use the OLD M[i], M[j] to update c (since the change Δc = di*(old ∂c/∂h[i]) + ...). If I update M first then use new M[i], I'd use new gradient (slightly off). The finite change in c from moving h[i] by di is exactly di*(the OLD M[i]) (the derivative at the OLD point) for the linear part, plus the -di² self. Using old M is correct. So I must use M[i], M[j] BEFORE M_update. Let me order: compute c_change = di*M[i] + dj*M[j] (using old M), then M_update, then c += c_change; c[L] -= di²+dj². But M_update modifies M in place; after it, M[i] is new. So capture c_change using old M first. Let me do:
```
ci = M[i].copy()? expensive. Instead:

M_update(M, i, di, j, dj, n)
c = c + dc
c[L] -= di*di + dj*dj
```
Since dc is computed before M_update, it uses old M rows → correct. And M_update modifies M in place (rows i and j get new). But note dc = di*M[i] + dj*M[j]: M[i] and M[j] are read (old) before update. Good. And I should not reuse dc after update. Fine.

Now, is one accept per i per sub-sweep enough to converge? Over sub-sweeps (up to maxsub per step), yes it iterates. Each sub-sweep = full pass over i doing at most one accept each. So per step we do up to maxsub full passes, each accepting up to n moves. Convergence in practice within a few passes per step. Good.

Let me now write:

```
import numpy as np
import time
from scipy.optimize import minimize

def compute_c5(h):
    return float(np.max(np.correlate(h, 1.0 - h, 'full')) * (2.0 / len(h)))

def project_h(h, n):
    target = 0.5 * n
    h = np.clip(np.asarray(h, dtype=float), 0.0, 1.0)
    for _ in range(2000):
        diff = target - h.sum()
        if abs(diff) < 1e-12:
            break
        if diff > 0:
            room = 1.0 - h
            tot = room.sum()
            if tot <= 1e-15:
                break
            h = np.clip(h + (diff / tot) * room, 0.0, 1.0)
        else:
            tot = h.sum()
            if tot <= 1e-15:
                break
            h = np.clip(h * (1.0 + diff / tot), 0.0, 1.0)
    diff = target - h.sum()
    if diff > 1e-12:
        idx = int(np.argmax(1.0 - h))
        h[idx] = h[idx] + min(diff, 1.0 - h[idx])
    elif diff < -1e-12:
        idx = int(np.argmax(h))
        h[idx] = h[idx] - min(-diff, h[idx])
    return h

def interp_resample(h0, n0, n1):
    if n0 == n1:
        return h0.astype(float).copy()
    x0 = (np.arange(n0) + 0.5) * (2.0 / n0)
    x1 = (np.arange(n1) + 0.5) * (2.0 / n1)
    return np.interp(x1, x0, h0)

def build_M(h):
    n = h.shape[0]
    L = n - 1
    t = np.arange(2 * n - 1) - L
    irows = np.arange(n)[:, None]
    im = irows - t[None, :]
    ip = irows + t[None, :]
    M = np.zeros((n, 2 * n - 1))
    imI = im.astype(int)
    ipI = ip.astype(int)
    m1 = (im >= 0) & (im < n)
    M[m1] += (1.0 - h[imI[m1]])
    m2 = (ip >= 0) & (ip < n)
    M[m2] -= h[ipI[m2]]
    return M

def M_update(M, i, di, j, dj, n):
    L = n - 1
    rows = np.arange(n)
    a = L + (rows - i)
    m = (a >= 0) & (a < 2 * n - 1)
    M[rows[m], a[m]] -= di
    b = L + (i - rows)
    m = (b >= 0) & (b < 2 * n - 1)
    M[rows[m], b[m]] -= di
    c1 = L + (rows - j)
    m = (c1 >= 0) & (c1 < 2 * n - 1)
    M[rows[m], c1[m]] -= dj
    d1 = L + (j - rows)
    m = (d1 >= 0) & (d1 < 2 * n - 1)
    M[rows[m], d1[m]] -= dj
    return M

def smax_obj(x, n, alpha):
    c = np.correlate(x, 1.0 - x, 'full')
    dx = 2.0 / n
    f = c * dx
    cm = float(f.max())
    w = f - cm
    keep = w > (-(40.0 / alpha))
    if not keep.any():
        return cm
    e = np.exp(alpha * w[keep])
    return cm + float(np.log(e.sum()) / alpha)

def smax_grad(x, n, alpha):
    M = build_M(x)
    c = np.correlate(x, 1.0 - x, 'full')
    dx = 2.0 / n
    f = c * dx
    cm = float(f.max())
    w = f - cm
    P = np.zeros(2 * n - 1)
    keep = w > (-(40.0 / alpha))
    if keep.any():
        e = np.exp(alpha * w[keep])
        P[keep] = e / e.sum()
    else:
        P[int(np.argmax(w))] = 1.0
    g = dx * (M @ P)
    return g

def csum_fun(x, n):
    return float(np.sum(x) - 0.5 * n)

def slsqp_stage(h, n, alpha, iters):
    try:
        cons = [{'type': 'eq', 'fun': csum_fun, 'args': (n,)}]
        bounds = [(0.0, 1.0)] * n
        res = minimize(smax_obj, h, args=(n, alpha), method='SLSQP', jac=smax_grad,
                       bounds=bounds, constraints=cons,
                       options={'maxiter': iters, 'ftol': 1e-12})
        hx = project_h(res.x, n)
        return hx, compute_c5(hx)
    except Exception:
        return h.compute_c5(h) if False else (h, compute_c5(h))
```
Oops typo. Let me fix return fallback: `return (h, compute_c5(h))`.

do_slsqp:
```
def do_slsqp(h, n, dead):
    h = project_h(h, n)
    best_h = h.copy(); best_c = compute_c5(h)
    alphas = [500, 1500, 6000, 20000]
    for a in alphas:
        if time.time() > dead:
            break
        iters = 45 if n <= 260 else (30 if n <= 340 else 20)
        h1, c1 = slsqp_stage(best_h, n, a, iters)   # warm from best so far
        if c1 < best_c - 1e-15:
            best_c = c1; best_h = h1
        else:
            # still set h to h1? keep best_h as warm; trying worse warm may hurt anneal; keep best_h
            h1_ = h1
        # continue annealing from best_h
    return best_h, best_c
```
Wait, annealing from best_h each stage vs from the last stage's output — for smooth-max, continuing from the last stage's output is the standard anneal. But if a stage went worse, continuing from the worse point could be bad. Let me continue from the last stage's output (h1) regardless, but track best. Actually SLSQP at increasing alpha expects the variable to be progressively tightened; starting each stage from the previous stage's result is right. Let me set h = h1 each stage (the chain), and track best. Let me do:
```
def do_slsqp(h, n, dead):
    h = project_h(h, n); cur = h; best_h = h.copy(); best_c = compute_c5(h)
    alphas = [500, 1500, 6000, 20000]
    for a in alphas:
        if time.time() > dead: break
        iters = 45 if n<=260 else (30 if n<=340 else 20)
        h1, c1 = slsqp_stage(cur, n, a, iters)
        cur = h1
        if c1 < best_c - 1e-15:
            best_c = c1; best_h = project_h(h1, n)
    return best_h, best_c
```
Good.

op_sequence_vec:
```
def op_sequence_vec(h, n, deadline, steps, maxsub):
    try:
        h = project_h(h, n)
        L = n - 1
        M = build_M(h)
        c = np.correlate(h, 1.0 - h, 'full')
        best = float(c.max())
        ci2 = c[None, :]
        for d in steps:
            if d <= 0:
                continue
            if time.time() > deadline:
                break
            subs = 0
            improved_any = True
            while improved_any and subs < maxsub and time.time() < deadline:
                improved_any = False
                subs += 1
                for i in range(n):
                    if time.time() > deadline:
                        return project_h(h, n), compute_c5(h)
                    jv = np.arange(i + 1, n)
                    if jv.size < 1:
                        continue
                    hj = h[jv]
                    fp = (hj >= d) and (h[i] <= 1.0 - d)
                    fn = (hj <= 1.0 - d) and (h[i] >= d)
                    if not (fp or fn):
                        continue
                    D = M[i][None, :] - M[jv]            # (J, C)
                    tp = fi * d   # placeholder? no
```
Let me just compute. Let me define:
```
                    C = M[jv]                    # (J, 2n-1)
                    row_i = M[i][None, :]
                    D = row_i - C                # M[i] - M[j]
                    base_p = c[None, :] + d * D
                    base_p[:, L] -= 2.0 * d * d
                    nmP = base_p.max(axis=1)
                    base_n = c[None, :] - d * D
                    base_n[:, L] -= 2.0 * d * d
                    nmN = base_n.max(axis=1)
                    nm = np.full(jv.size, np.inf)
                    if fp:
                        nm = np.where(hj >= d, nmP, nm)   # fp requires hj>=d (and h[i]<=1-d)
                    if fn:
                        nm = np.where(hj <= 1.0 - d, np.minimum(nm, nmN), nm)
                    # but fp/fn scalars require h[i] conditions already checked via 'if not(fp or fn) continue'
                    order = np.argsort(nm)
                    chosen = -1; idx = -1; dirsign = 1
                    for kk in order:
                        val = nm[kk]
                        if val < best - 1e-14:
                            chosen = int(jv[kk]); idx = int(kk)
                            p_ok = (hj[idx] >= d)
                            n_ok = (hj[idx] <= 1.0 - d)
                            if p_ok and (not n_ok or nmP[idx] <= nmN[idx]):
                                dirsign = 1
                            else:
                                dirsign = -1
                            break
                    if chosen < 0:
                        continue
                    ii = i; jj = chosen
                    if dirsign > 0:
                        di = d; dj = -d
                    else:
                        di = -d; dj = d
                    dc = di * M[ii] + dj * M[jj]
                    M_update(M, ii, di, jj, dj, n)
                    c = c + dc
                    c[L] -= di * di + dj * dj
                    h[ii] += di
                    h[jj] += dj
                    best = float(c.max())
                    improved_any = True
        h = project_h(h, n)
        return h, compute_c5(h)
    except Exception:
        h = project_h(h, n)
        return h, compute_c5(h)
```

 For -d: need h[i]>=d (i-level) and hj<=1-d (per j).

Now golden_m:
```
def golden_m(h, n, deadline, rounds):
    try:
        h = project_h(h, n)
        M = build_M(h); c = np.correlate(h, 1.0 - h, 'full'); L = n - 1; best = float(c.max())
        ips = (5.0 ** 0.5 - 1.0) / 2.0
        def mm(cc, DD, t):
            e = cc + t * DD
            e[L] = e[L] - 2.0 * t * t
            return float(e.max())
```
Wait, mm uses c (baseline) — I pass c as first arg but it's the current c. Golden D = M[i]-M[jj] (rows). new_max at t: max(c + t*D) with -2t² at L. Let me define mm(D, t) using the OUTER c and L. But mm as a nested def = closure → not allowed (no closures). I'll inline or make a top-level helper `pair_newmax(c, D, t, L)`. Let me make top-level:
```
def pair_newmax(c, D, t, L):
    e = c + t * D
    e[L] = e[L] - 2.0 * t * t
    return float(e.max())
```
golden_m:
```
def golden_m(h, n, deadline, rounds):
    try:
        h = project_h(h, n)
        M = build_M(h); c = np.correlate(h, 1.0 - h, 'full'); L = n - 1; best = float(c.max())
        ips = (5.0 ** 0.5 - 1.0) / 2.0
        for _ in range(rounds):
            if time.time() > deadline:
                break
            improved = False
            for i in range(n):
                if time.time() > deadline:
                    break
                for jj in range(i + 1, n):
                    if time.time() > deadline:
                        break
                    lo = max(-h[i], h[jj] - 1.0)
                    hi = min(1.0 - h[i], h[jj])
                    if hi - lo < 1e-8:
                        continue
                    D = M[i] - M[jj]
                    a2 = lo + ips * (hi - lo)
                    b2 = hi - ips * (hi - lo)
                    fa = pair_newmax(c, D, a2, L)
                    fb = pair_newmax(c, D, b2, L)
                    for _g in range(12):
                        if fa < fb:
                            hi = b2; b2 = a2; fb = fa
                            a2 = lo + ips * (hi - lo); fa = pair_newmax(c, D, a2, L)
                        else:
                            lo = a2; a2 = b2; fa = fb
                            b2 = hi - ips * (hi - lo); fb = pair_newmax(c, D, b2, L)
                    t = 0.5 * (lo + hi)
                    ft = pair_newmax(c, D, t, L)
                    if ft < best - 1e-13:
                        di = t; dj = -t
                        dc = di * M[i] + dj * M[jj]
                        M_update(M, i, di, jj, dj, n)
                        c = c + dc
                        c[L] -= di * di + dj * dj
                        h[i] += di; h[jj] += dj
                        best = float(c.max()); improved = True
            if not improved:
                break
        h = project_h(h, n)
        return h, compute_c5(h)
    except Exception:
        h = project_h(h, n)
        return h, compute_c5(h)
```
golden is O(n² × 12) numpy calls — for n≤300 ~28800×12=345k/round. Maybe 1-3s/round. Rounds 2-3. OK for n≤300.

optimize_node:
```
def optimize_node(h0, n, nd, use_slsqp, heavy_op):
    h = project_h(h0, n)
    cs0 = compute_c5(h)
    if use_slsqp:
        sl_dead = time.time() + 0.32 * max(1.0, nd - time.time())
        sl_dead = min(sl_dead, nd - 3)
        if sl_dead - time.time() > 3:
            hs, cs = do_slsqp(h, n, sl_dead)
            if cs < cs0 - 1e-15:
                h = hs; cs0 = cs
    steps = steps_for(n)
    maxsub = maxsub_for(n)
    op_dead = time.time() + 0.60 * max(1.0, nd - time.time())
    op_dead = min(op_dead, nd - 2)
    h, cs = op_sequence_vec(h, n, op_dead, steps, maxsub)
    if n <= 300 and time.time() < nd - 4:
        gr = 3 if n <= 240 else (2 if n <= 300 else 0)
        gl_dead = min(nd - 1, time.time() + (nd - time.time()) * 0.4)
        h, cs = golden_m(h, n, gl_dead, gr)
    h = project_h(h, n); cs = compute_c5(h)
    return h, cs
```
steps_for/maxsub_for:
```
def steps_for(n):
    if n <= 260:
        return [0.4, 0.2, 0.1, 0.05, 0.02, 0.01, 0.005, 0.002, 0.001, 0.0005, 0.0002]
    return [0.3, 0.15, 0.07, 0.03, 0.015, 0.007, 0.0035, 0.0018, 0.001, 0.0006]
def maxsub_for(n):
    if n <= 240:
        return 26
    if n <= 320:
        return 16
    return 9
```

def run(seed=42, budget_s=1100, **kwargs):
    t0 = time.time()
    try:
        run_dead = t0 + float(budget_s) - 12.0
    except Exception:
        run_dead = t0 + 108.0
    rng = np.random.RandomState(seed)
    # parent
    try:
        pah = np.array(initial_h_values, dtype=float).copy()
        pn = len(pah)
        if pn < 8:
            pah = np.full(200, 0.5); pn = 200
    except Exception:
        pah = np.full(200, 0.5); pn = 200
    pah = project_h(pah, pn)
    bh = pah.copy()
    bc = compute_c5(pah)
    bn = pn
    nodes = [(200, 110), (240, 100), (280, 110), (320, 120), (360, 135), (400, 150)]
    for (n, cap) in nodes:
        if time.time() > run_dead - 60:
            break
        h_in = nh if bn != n else bh.copy()
        nd = min(time.time() + cap, run_dead - 60)
        if nd - time.time() < 12:
            continue
        topt = optimize_node(h_in, n, nd, True, True)
        if topt[1] < bc - 1e-15:
            bc = topt[1]; bh = topt[0]; bn = n
    # extra fine
    for n in [440, 470]:
        if time.time() > run_dead - 70:
            break
        h_in = interp_resample(bh, bn, n) if bn != n else bh.copy()
        nd = min(time.time() + 110, run_dead - 70)
        if nd - time.time() < 15:
            continue
        topt = optimize_node(h_in, n, nd, True, True)
        if topt[1] < bc - 1e-15:
            bc = topt[1]; bh = topt[0]; bn = n
    # restarts at best
    for s in [0.02, 0.04, 0.08, 0.12]:
        if time.time() > run_dead - 45:
            break
        hp = bh + rng.normal(0, s, bn)
        hp = project_h(hp, bn)
        nd = min(time.time() + 55, run_dead - 45)
        if nd - time.time() < 12:
            continue
        topt = optimize_node(hp, bn, nd, False, True)
        if topt[1] < bc - 1e-15:
            bc = topt[1]; bh = topt[0]
    # final micro polish
    if time.time() < run_dead - 8:
        fh = op_sequence_vec(bh, bn, min(time.time() + 30, run_dead - 8), [0.001, 0.0005, 0.0002, 0.0001], 5)
        fc = compute_c5(fh) if False else fh[1]
```

    # final exact
    bh = project_h(bh, bn)
